<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HS4AEB25qMUQ4B5HMrPUAP`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Threshold Regime Analysis}\label{sec:theory_scaling}
2	
3	\subsection{Entity Construction as MBR Decoding}\label{sec:mbr_theory}
4	
5	Entity-level construction selects $\hat{y}_{\textup{MBR}} = \{e \in E_\cup : \hat{p}(e) > \alpha/(\alpha{+}1)\}$ under balanced set-overlap utility; $\alpha{=}1$ recovers majority vote (Proposition~\ref{prop:construction_mbr}; Table~\ref{tab:entity_construction}; Appendix~\ref{app:mbr_proof}).
6	Signal-derived weights $w_i$ yield quality-weighted MBR: $\hat{p}_w(e) = \sum_{i} w_i \, \mathbf{1}[e {\in} y_i] / \sum_{i} w_i$; weighting improves when $w_i$ correlates with instance quality but degrades otherwise (LP on SciERC: $\rho{\approx}0.2$; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}), consistent with utility degradation on structured outputs \cite{eikema2025structured}.
7	We select $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ at the elbow of the compression curve, where LP-best selection advantage transitions from $+1.5$\,pp (compressed) to $+4.6$\,pp (non-compressed; Appendix~\ref{app:fewnerd_epsilon}).
8	
9	\subsection{Scaling and Inverse Scaling}\label{sec:scaling}
10	
11	Oracle F1 follows $F_1(N) = a - b \cdot N^{-c}$ ($R^2 > 0.999$; Figure~\ref{fig:oracle_scaling}): Few-NERD ($c{\approx}0.61$) scales comparably to reasoning tasks \cite{brown2024monkeys}, SciERC converges more slowly ($c{\approx}0.35$), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\,pp---a persistent gap.
12	The gap persists across scales and families: scaling from 8B to 14B FT increases oracle headroom ($+1.2$\,pp) with minimal greedy gain ($+0.9$\,pp), and GPT-5.5 (200 Few-NERD instances, zero-shot) exhibits the same pattern---50\% degeneracy, SJ $\rho{=}0.346$, MV $+1.4$\,pp yet 6.8\,pp oracle headroom unrealized.\footnote{Of the 10.1\% of GPT-5.5 samples producing empty entity sets, 88\% correspond to gold-empty instances; true parse failures affect only 1.2\% of samples (19/1{,}600) and shift all reported metrics by ${<}0.5$\,pp. See Appendix~\ref{app:frontier_sc}.}
13	Degeneracy is model-family-dependent rather than scale-dependent (Qwen2.5: $31.7$--$46.2$\% vs.\ Qwen3: $13.0$\% vs.\ GPT-5.5: $50$\% on Few-NERD; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale}).
14	
15	\begin{figure}[t]
16	\centering
17	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_oracle_scaling.pdf}
18	\caption{Oracle $F_1$ as a function of sample count $N$, fitted with $F_1(N) = a - b \cdot N^{-c}$. All fits $R^2 > 0.999$. Horizontal dashed lines: greedy ($T{=}0$) F1. The ceiling grows monotonically with $N$, but the correlation-selection gap (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}) limits how much is actually recoverable.}
19	\label{fig:oracle_scaling}
20	\end{figure}
21	
22	\paragraph{Inverse scaling under adaptive thresholds.}
23	Increasing $N$ \textit{decreases} construction F1 in 40 of 58 transitions ($p{<}0.05$; Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
24	Methods using $\theta{=}2/N$ are most affected ($-3.09$\,pp at $N{=}8$, $-6.90$\,pp at $N{=}16$ vs.\ greedy on Few-NERD); majority vote ($\theta{=}\tfrac{1}{2}$) reduces the delta to $-0.87$/$-0.60$\,pp---severity reduction, not immunity (Table~\ref{tab:theta_sweep}).
25	Inverse scaling concentrates in non-degenerate instances ($11.1$\% vs.\ $1.3$\% for degenerate; OR${=}0.109$; Appendix~\ref{app:degen_inverse}).
26	Few-shot demonstrations improve QE (CoNLL SJ $\rho$ $.30{\to}.44$) but not construction (all n.s.; Appendix~\ref{app:fs_error}).
27	
28	\subsection{Degeneracy Decomposition}\label{sec:theory}
29	
30	\noindent\textbf{Degeneracy Decomposition.}\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}
31	For any aggregation method $A$: $\mathbb{E}[\Delta F_1(A)] \leq (1 - D) \cdot G_{\textup{nd}}$,
32	where $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.
33	For signal-based methods with alignment $\alpha \in [0,1]$, this tightens to $(1{-}D) \cdot G_{\textup{nd}} \cdot \alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:bound_proof}).
34	
35	\noindent The bound is an intentionally loose \textit{necessary condition test}: when $D{\to}1$ or $\alpha{\to}0$, no aggregation method can improve over greedy, regardless of implementation.
36	It identifies \textit{where} aggregation fails---screening for favorable conditions---rather than quantitatively predicting gains.
37	Across 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound; the low recovery ratio (3--12\%; Figure~\ref{fig:bound}) reflects its role as a screening test, not a tight estimate.
38	$\alpha$---not $D$---is the binding constraint ($\alpha{=}0.000$--$0.161$; $8$--$33{\times}$ tighter), and this conclusion is robust to signal combination (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}), verifier-based construction (\S\ref{sec:verifier}), and $\theta_{\text{crit}} \in [0.60, 0.80]$.
39	
40	\begin{figure}[t]
41	\centering
42	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_bound}
43	\caption{SC gain vs.\ degeneracy across 16 configurations (SciERC-specific $\alpha$). Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\theta_{\text{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\varepsilon{=}0.3$\,pp. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}
44	\label{fig:bound}
45	\end{figure}
46	
47	\paragraph{Bound looseness.}
48	The decomposition is a necessary condition screen showing how degeneracy and alignment jointly suppress gains, not a quantitative prediction tool. Two sources of looseness dominate: (1)~the entity independence assumption---split-half correlation $r{=}0.395$--$0.578$ (Table~\ref{tab:fk_independence}) reveals substantial inter-entity correlation structure within instances, which the bound ignores; (2)~$\alpha$ is estimated conservatively as the minimum across instances rather than an instance-adaptive value, further widening the gap. Together these explain the 3--12\% recovery ratio (bound ratio $0.13$--$0.63$). The decomposition nonetheless identifies $\alpha{\to}0$, not degeneracy, as the binding constraint across all tested configurations.
49	
50	MV improves F1 only if marginal-entity precision exceeds $F_1^{\textup{gr}}/2$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:entity_selection_bound}; Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_crit}).

      "content": "\\section{Threshold Regime Analysis}\\label{sec:theory_scaling}\n\n\\subsection{Entity Construction as MBR Decoding}\\label{sec:mbr_theory}\n\nEntity-level construction selects $\\hat{y}_{\\textup{MBR}} = \\{e \\in E_\\cup : \\hat{p}(e) > \\alpha/(\\alpha{+}1)\\}$ under balanced set-overlap utility; $\\alpha{=}1$ recovers majority vote (Proposition~\\ref{prop:construction_mbr}; Table~\\ref{tab:entity_construction}; Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_proof}).\nSignal-derived weights $w_i$ yield quality-weighted MBR: $\\hat{p}_w(e) = \\sum_{i} w_i \\, \\mathbf{1}[e {\\in} y_i] / \\sum_{i} w_i$; weighting improves when $w_i$ correlates with instance quality but degrades otherwise (LP on SciERC: $\\rho{\\approx}0.2$; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}), consistent with utility degradation on structured outputs \\cite{eikema2025structured}.\nWe select $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ at the elbow of the compression curve, where LP-best selection advantage transitions from $+1.5$\\,pp (compressed) to $+4.6$\\,pp (non-compressed; Appendix~\\ref{app:fewnerd_epsilon}).\n\n\\subsection{Scaling and Inverse Scaling}\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nOracle F1 follows $F_1(N) = a - b \\cdot N^{-c}$ ($R^2 > 0.999$; Figure~\\ref{fig:oracle_scaling}): Few-NERD ($c{\\approx}0.61$) scales comparably to reasoning tasks \\cite{brown2024monkeys}, SciERC converges more slowly ($c{\\approx}0.35$), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\\,pp---a persistent gap.\nThe gap persists across scales and families: scaling from 8B to 14B FT increases oracle headroom ($+1.2$\\,pp) with minimal greedy gain ($+0.9$\\,pp), and GPT-5.5 (200 Few-NERD instances, zero-shot) exhibits the same pattern---50\\% degeneracy, SJ $\\rho{=}0.346$, MV $+1.4$\\,pp yet 6.8\\,pp oracle headroom unrealized.\\footnote{Of the 10.1\\% of GPT-5.5 samples producing empty entity sets, 88\\% correspond to gold-empty instances; true parse failures affect only 1.2\\% of samples (19/1{,}600) and shift all reported metrics by ${<}0.5$\\,pp. See Appendix~\\ref{app:frontier_sc}.}\nDegeneracy is model-family-dependent rather than scale-dependent (Qwen2.5: $31.7$--$46.2$\\% vs.\\ Qwen3: $13.0$\\% vs.\\ GPT-5.5: $50$\\% on Few-NERD; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_oracle_scaling.pdf}\n\\caption{Oracle $F_1$ as a function of sample count $N$, fitted with $F_1(N) = a - b \\cdot N^{-c}$. All fits $R^2 > 0.999$. Horizontal dashed lines: greedy ($T{=}0$) F1. The ceiling grows monotonically with $N$, but the correlation-selection gap (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}) limits how much is actually recoverable.}\n\\label{fig:oracle_scaling}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inverse scaling under adaptive thresholds.}\nIncreasing $N$ \\textit{decreases} construction F1 in 40 of 58 transitions ($p{<}0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nMethods using $\\theta{=}2/N$ are most affected ($-3.09$\\,pp at $N{=}8$, $-6.90$\\,pp at $N{=}16$ vs.\\ greedy on Few-NERD); majority vote ($\\theta{=}\\tfrac{1}{2}$) reduces the delta to $-0.87$/$-0.60$\\,pp---severity reduction, not immunity (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_sweep}).\nInverse scaling concentrates in non-degenerate instances ($11.1$\\% vs.\\ $1.3$\\% for degenerate; OR${=}0.109$; Appendix~\\ref{app:degen_inverse}).\nFew-shot demonstrations improve QE (CoNLL SJ $\\rho$ $.30{\\to}.44$) but not construction (all n.s.; Appendix~\\ref{app:fs_error}).\n\n\\subsection{Degeneracy Decomposition}\\label{sec:theory}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Degeneracy Decomposition.}\\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}\nFor any aggregation method $A$: $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1(A)] \\leq (1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}}$,\nwhere $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.\nFor signal-based methods with alignment $\\alpha \\in [0,1]$, this tightens to $(1{-}D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:bound_proof}).\n\n\\noindent The bound is an intentionally loose \\textit{necessary condition test}: when $D{\\to}1$ or $\\alpha{\\to}0$, no aggregation method can improve over greedy, regardless of implementation.\nIt identifies \\textit{where} aggregation fails---screening for favorable conditions---rather than quantitatively predicting gains.\nAcross 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound; the low recovery ratio (3--12\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:bound}) reflects its role as a screening test, not a tight estimate.\n$\\alpha$---not $D$---is the binding constraint ($\\alpha{=}0.000$--$0.161$; $8$--$33{\\times}$ tighter), and this conclusion is robust to signal combination (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_main}), verifier-based construction (\\S\\ref{sec:verifier}), and $\\theta_{\\text{crit}} \\in [0.60, 0.80]$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_bound}\n\\caption{SC gain vs.\\ degeneracy across 16 configurations (SciERC-specific $\\alpha$). Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\\theta_{\\text{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}\n\\label{fig:bound}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Bound looseness.}\nThe decomposition is a necessary condition screen showing how degeneracy and alignment jointly suppress gains, not a quantitative prediction tool. Two sources of looseness dominate: (1)~the entity independence assumption---split-half correlation $r{=}0.395$--$0.578$ (Table~\\ref{tab:fk_independence}) reveals substantial inter-entity correlation structure within instances, which the bound ignores; (2)~$\\alpha$ is estimated conservatively as the minimum across instances rather than an instance-adaptive value, further widening the gap. 2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Correlation-selection gap**: QE signals predict quality (ρ up to 0.45, AUROC 0.82) but selection yields <1pp gain
   - **Degeneracy decomposition**: E[ΔF1(A)] ≤ (1-D)·G_nd·α; α is binding constraint (8-33× tighter than D)
   - **Three-stage contribution**: Discover → Diagnose → Construct (exploratory)
   - **Instance-averaged vs micro-F1 distinction**: Critical metric difference (Few-NERD: 0.748 instance-averaged vs 0.791 micro-F1 for 3-epoch greedy)
   - **Entity-set vs constant-F1 degeneracy**: Two different definitions giving different rates (8.8%/26.4% vs 13%/31% for Few-NERD epoch confound)
   - **Statistical corrections**: Global Holm k=57 p_adj=0.055; per-family k=10 p_adj=0.006
   - **N-scaling pretrained results**: MV Δ plateau at N=32 (+3.85pp vs N=16 +4.06pp), oracle headroom grows (+16.56pp), capture rate drops (19.04%)
   - **EMNLP 2026 page limit**: Strict 8-page main text; Limitations/Ethics don't count

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **theory.tex** (52 lines, main text §6):
     - Contains MBR theory (§6.1), scaling/inverse scaling (§6.2), degeneracy decomposition (§6.3)

{
  "parentUuid": "c8afc73f-c98a-4946-8455-36bed7f53841",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/theory.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/theory.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Threshold Regime Analysis}\\label{sec:theory_scaling}\n\n\\subsection{Entity Construction as MBR Decoding}\\label{sec:mbr_theory}\n\nEntity-level construction selects $\\hat{y}_{\\textup{MBR}} = \\{e \\in E_\\cup : \\hat{p}(e) > \\alpha/(\\alpha{+}1)\\}$ under balanced set-overlap utility; $\\alpha{=}1$ recovers majority vote (Proposition~\\ref{prop:construction_mbr}; Table~\\ref{tab:entity_construction}; Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_proof}).\nSignal-derived weights $w_i$ yield quality-weighted MBR: $\\hat{p}_w(e) = \\sum_{i} w_i \\, \\mathbf{1}[e {\\in} y_i] / \\sum_{i} w_i$; weighting improves when $w_i$ correlates with instance quality but degrades otherwise (LP on SciERC: $\\rho{\\approx}0.2$; \\S\\ref{sec:prescriptive}), consistent with utility degradation on structured outputs \\cite{eikema2025structured}.\nWe select $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ at the elbow of the compression curve, where LP-best selection advantage transitions from $+1.5$\\,pp (compressed) to $+4.6$\\,pp (non-compressed; Appendix~\\ref{app:fewnerd_epsilon}).\n\n\\subsection{Scaling and Inverse Scaling}\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nOracle F1 follows $F_1(N) = a - b \\cdot N^{-c}$ ($R^2 > 0.999$; Figure~\\ref{fig:oracle_scaling}): Few-NERD ($c{\\approx}0.61$) scales comparably to reasoning tasks \\cite{brown2024monkeys}, SciERC converges more slowly ($c{\\approx}0.35$), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\\,pp---a persistent gap.\nThe gap persists across scales and families: scaling from 8B to 14B FT increases oracle headroom ($+1.2$\\,pp) with minimal greedy gain ($+0.9$\\,pp), and GPT-5.5 (200 Few-NERD instances, zero-shot) exhibits the same pattern---50\\% degeneracy, SJ $\\rho{=}0.346$, MV $+1.4$\\,pp yet 6.8\\,pp oracle headroom unrealized.\\footnote{Of the 10.1\\% of GPT-5.5 samples producing empty entity sets, 88\\% correspond to gold-empty instances; true parse failures affect only 1.2\\% of samples (19/1{,}600) and shift all reported metrics by ${<}0.5$\\,pp. See Appendix~\\ref{app:frontier_sc}.}\nDegeneracy is model-family-dependent rather than scale-dependent (Qwen2.5: $31.7$--$46.2$\\% vs.\\ Qwen3: $13.0$\\% vs.\\ GPT-5.5: $50$\\% on Few-NERD; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_oracle_scaling.pdf}\n\\caption{Oracle $F_1$ as a function of sample count $N$, fitted with $F_1(N) = a - b \\cdot N^{-c}$. All fits $R^2 > 0.999$. Horizontal dashed lines: greedy ($T{=}0$) F1. The ceiling grows monotonically with $N$, but the correlation-selection gap (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}) limits how much is actually recoverable.}\n\\label{fig:oracle_scaling}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inverse scaling under adaptive thresholds.}\nIncreasing $N$ \\textit{decreases} construction F1 in 40 of 58 transitions ($p{<}0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nMethods using $\\theta{=}2/N$ are most affected ($-3.09$\\,pp at $N{=}8$, $-6.90$\\,pp at $N{=}16$ vs.\\ greedy on Few-NERD); majority vote ($\\theta{=}\\tfrac{1}{2}$) reduces the delta to $-0.87$/$-0.60$\\,pp---severity reduction, not immunity (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_sweep}).\nInverse scaling concentrates in non-degenerate instances ($11.1$\\% vs.\\ $1.3$\\% for degenerate; OR${=}0.109$; Appendix~\\ref{app:degen_inverse}).\nFew-shot demonstrations improve QE (CoNLL SJ $\\rho$ $.30{\\to}.44$) but not construction (all n.s.; Appendix~\\ref{app:fs_error}).\n\n\\subsection{Degeneracy Decomposition}\\label{sec:theory}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Degeneracy Decomposition.}\\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}\nFor any aggregation method $A$: $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1(A)] \\leq (1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}}$,\nwhere $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.\nFor signal-based methods with alignment $\\alpha \\in [0,1]$, this tightens to $(1{-}D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:bound_proof}).\n\n\\noindent The bound is an intentionally loose \\textit{necessary condition test}: when $D{\\to}1$ or $\\alpha{\\to}0$, no aggregation method can improve over greedy, regardless of implementation.\nIt identifies \\textit{where} aggregation fails---screening for favorable conditions---rather than quantitatively predicting gains.\nAcross 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound; the low recovery ratio (3--12\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:bound}) reflects its role as a screening test, not a tight estimate.\n$\\alpha$---not $D$---is the binding constraint ($\\alpha{=}0.000$--$0.161$; $8$--$33{\\times}$ tighter), and this conclusion is robust to signal combination (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_main}), verifier-based construction (\\S\\ref{sec:verifier}), and $\\theta_{\\text{crit}} \\in [0.60, 0.80]$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_bound}\n\\caption{SC gain vs.\\ degeneracy across 16 configurations (SciERC-specific $\\alpha$). Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\\theta_{\\text{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}\n\\label{fig:bound}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Bound looseness.}\nThe decomposition is a necessary condition screen showing how degeneracy and alignment jointly suppress gains, not a quantitative prediction tool. Two sources of looseness dominate: (1)~the entity independence assumption---split-half correlation $r{=}0.395$--$0.578$ (Table~\\ref{tab:fk_independence}) reveals substantial inter-entity correlation structure within instances, which the bound ignores; (2)~$\\alpha$ is estimated conservatively as the minimum across instances rather than an instance-adaptive value, further widening the gap. Together these explain the 3--12\\% recovery ratio (bound ratio $0.13$--$0.63$). The decomposition nonetheless identifies $\\alpha{\\to}0$, not degeneracy, as the binding constraint across all tested configurations.\n\nMV improves F1 only if marginal-entity precision exceeds $F_1^{\\textup{gr}}/2$ (Proposition~\\ref{prop:entity_selection_bound}; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_crit}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:alpha_crit} validates: at $N{=}8$, no dataset meets this condition ($\\Delta$ from $-2.74$ to ${\\approx}0$\\,pp); CoNLL ($\\alpha_{\\textup{crit,eff}}{=}1.148 {>} 1$) makes MV gain structurally impossible.\n",
        "numLines": 52,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 52
      }
    },

  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QCrtGuK89buxDsfDkqoFS7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}\label{sec:selection_method}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	\paragraph{When test-time scaling fails for NER.}
4	For discrete-answer reasoning, sample diversity reflects diverse reasoning paths; for NER, degeneracy ($12$--$68$\%) reduces candidate diversity while signal alignment collapse ($\alpha{\to}0$) limits ranking.
5	The degeneracy decomposition (\S\ref{sec:theory}) identifies $\alpha$---not degeneracy---as the binding constraint ($8$--$33{\times}$ tighter); this persists across families \cite{yun2025price}, scales (4B--72B), and decoding modes (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).
6	The diagnostic framework---identifying these co-occurring conditions---is our primary contribution.
7	At $N{=}8$, high degeneracy ($>50$\%) suggests scaling will not help; low degeneracy with strong LP-F1 alignment may warrant exploratory entity construction; otherwise, greedy decoding remains the default.
8	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DqCA1CQTnQVaei329X9uDi`</summary>

```text
1	Our primary evidence is from English NER with fine-tuned 7--14B open-weight models; frontier models exhibit markedly different degeneracy profiles (5.5--74\%; Table~\ref{tab:frontier_sc_cross}), and cross-task extensions (RE $n{=}397$, SQL $n{=}196$) are exploratory.
2	The framework is diagnostic, not causal: under global Holm--Bonferroni ($k{=}57$), the strongest construction claim reaches $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.055$; our negative selection claims are bounded by statistical power (2--3 seeds, MDE ${\approx}1.5$\,pp).
3	Entity-level construction requires $N{\times}$ inference cost for modest gains ($+1$--$1.4$\,pp), and we use a single prompt template per task.
4	
```

Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. ```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:discussion}\label{sec:selection_method}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	\paragraph{When test-time scaling fails for NER.}
4	For discrete-answer reasoning, sample diversity reflects diverse reasoning paths; for NER, degeneracy ($12$--$68$\%) reduces candidate diversity while signal alignment collapse ($\alpha{\to}0$) limits ranking.
5	The degeneracy decomposition (\S\ref{sec:theory}) identifies $\alpha$---not degeneracy---as the binding constraint ($8$--$33{\times}$ tighter); this persists across families \cite{yun2025price}, scales (4B--72B), and decoding modes (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).
6	The diagnostic framework---identifying these co-occurring conditions---is our primary contribution.
7	At $N{=}8$, high degeneracy ($>50$\%) suggests scaling will not help; low degeneracy with strong LP-F1 alignment may warrant exploratory entity construction; otherwise, greedy decoding remains the default.
8	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012DL9Wbj1VQJzzxpce9PBhr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Threshold Regime Analysis}\label{sec:theory_scaling}
2	
3	\subsection{Entity Construction as MBR Decoding}\label{sec:mbr_theory}
4	
5	Entity-level construction selects $\hat{y}_{\textup{MBR}} = \{e \in E_\cup : \hat{p}(e) > \alpha/(\alpha{+}1)\}$ under balanced set-overlap utility; $\alpha{=}1$ recovers majority vote (Proposition~\ref{prop:construction_mbr}; Table~\ref{tab:entity_construction}; Appendix~\ref{app:mbr_proof}).
6	Signal-derived weights $w_i$ yield quality-weighted MBR: $\hat{p}_w(e) = \sum_{i} w_i \, \mathbf{1}[e {\in} y_i] / \sum_{i} w_i$; weighting improves when $w_i$ correlates with instance quality but degrades otherwise (LP on SciERC: $\rho{\approx}0.2$; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}), consistent with utility degradation on structured outputs \cite{eikema2025structured}.
7	We select $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ at the elbow of the compression curve, where LP-best selection advantage transitions from $+1.5$\,pp (compressed) to $+4.6$\,pp (non-compressed; Appendix~\ref{app:fewnerd_epsilon}).
8	
9	\subsection{Scaling and Inverse Scaling}\label{sec:scaling}
10	
11	Oracle F1 follows $F_1(N) = a - b \cdot N^{-c}$ ($R^2 > 0.999$; Figure~\ref{fig:oracle_scaling}): Few-NERD ($c{\approx}0.61$) scales comparably to reasoning tasks \cite{brown2024monkeys}, SciERC converges more slowly ($c{\approx}0.35$), yet confidence-based aggregation recovers ${<}1$\,pp---a persistent gap.
12	The gap persists across scales and families: scaling from 8B to 14B FT increases oracle headroom ($+1.2$\,pp) with minimal greedy gain ($+0.9$\,pp), and GPT-5.5 (200 Few-NERD instances, zero-shot) exhibits the same pattern---50\% degeneracy, SJ $\rho{=}0.346$, MV $+1.4$\,pp yet 6.8\,pp oracle headroom unrealized.\footnote{Of the 10.1\% of GPT-5.5 samples producing empty entity sets, 88\% correspond to gold-empty instances; true parse failures affect only 1.2\% of samples (19/1{,}600) and shift all reported metrics by ${<}0.5$\,pp. See Appendix~\ref{app:frontier_sc}.}
13	Degeneracy is model-family-dependent rather than scale-dependent (Qwen2.5: $31.7$--$46.2$\% vs.\ Qwen3: $13.0$\% vs.\ GPT-5.5: $50$\% on Few-NERD; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale}).
14	
15	\begin{figure}[t]
16	\centering
17	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_oracle_scaling.pdf}
18	\caption{Oracle $F_1$ as a function of sample count $N$, fitted with $F_1(N) = a - b \cdot N^{-c}$. All fits $R^2 > 0.999$. Horizontal dashed lines: greedy ($T{=}0$) F1. The ceiling grows monotonically with $N$, but the correlation-selection gap (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}) limits how much is actually recoverable.}
19	\label{fig:oracle_scaling}
20	\end{figure}
21	
22	\paragraph{Inverse scaling under adaptive thresholds.}
23	Increasing $N$ \textit{decreases} construction F1 in 40 of 58 transitions ($p{<}0.05$; Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
24	Methods using $\theta{=}2/N$ are most affected ($-3.09$\,pp at $N{=}8$, $-6.90$\,pp at $N{=}16$ vs.\ greedy on Few-NERD); majority vote ($\theta{=}\tfrac{1}{2}$) reduces the delta to $-0.87$/$-0.60$\,pp---severity reduction, not immunity (Table~\ref{tab:theta_sweep}).
25	Inverse scaling concentrates in non-degenerate instances ($11.1$\% vs.\ $1.3$\% for degenerate; OR${=}0.109$; Appendix~\ref{app:degen_inverse}).
26	Few-shot demonstrations improve QE (CoNLL SJ $\rho$ $.30{\to}.44$) but not construction (all n.s.; Appendix~\ref{app:fs_error}).
27	
28	\subsection{Degeneracy Decomposition}\label{sec:theory}
29	
30	\noindent\textbf{Degeneracy Decomposition.}\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}
31	For any aggregation method $A$: $\mathbb{E}[\Delta F_1(A)] \leq (1 - D) \cdot G_{\textup{nd}}$,
32	where $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.
33	For signal-based methods with alignment $\alpha \in [0,1]$, this tightens to $(1{-}D) \cdot G_{\textup{nd}} \cdot \alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:bound_proof}).
34	
35	\noindent The bound is an intentionally loose \textit{necessary condition test}: when $D{\to}1$ or $\alpha{\to}0$, no aggregation method can improve over greedy, regardless of implementation.
36	It identifies \textit{where} aggregation fails---screening for favorable conditions---rather than quantitatively predicting gains.
37	Across 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound; the low recovery ratio (3--12\%; Figure~\ref{fig:bound}) reflects its role as a screening test, not a tight estimate.
38	$\alpha$---not $D$---is the binding constraint ($\alpha{=}0.000$--$0.121$; $8$--$33{\times}$ tighter), and this conclusion is robust to signal combination (\S\ref{sec:robustness_main}), verifier-based construction (\S\ref{sec:verifier}), and $\theta_{\text{crit}} \in [0.60, 0.80]$.
39	
40	\begin{figure}[t]
41	\centering
42	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_bound}
43	\caption{SC gain vs.\ degeneracy across 16 configurations (SciERC-specific $\alpha$). Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\theta_{\text{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\varepsilon{=}0.3$\,pp. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}
44	\label{fig:bound}
45	\end{figure}
46	
47	\paragraph{Bound looseness.}
48	The decomposition is a necessary condition screen showing how degeneracy and alignment jointly suppress gains, not a quantitative prediction tool. Two sources of looseness dominate: (1)~the entity independence assumption---split-half correlation $r{=}0.395$--$0.578$ (Table~\ref{tab:fk_independence}) reveals substantial inter-entity correlation structure within instances, which the bound ignores; (2)~$\alpha$ is estimated conservatively as the minimum across instances rather than an instance-adaptive value, further widening the gap. Together these explain the 3--12\% recovery ratio (bound ratio $0.13$--$0.63$). The decomposition nonetheless identifies $\alpha{\to}0$, not degeneracy, as the binding constraint across all tested configurations.
49	
50	MV improves F1 only if marginal-entity precision exceeds $F_1^{\textup{gr}}/2$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:entity_selection_bound}; Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_crit}).
51	Table~\ref{tab:alpha_crit} validates: at $N{=}8$, no dataset meets this condition ($\Delta$ from $-2.74$ to ${\approx}0$\,pp); CoNLL ($\alpha_{\textup{crit,eff}}{=}1.148 {>} 1$) makes MV gain structurally impossible.
52	
```

The ceiling grows monotonically with $N$, but the correlation-selection gap (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}) limits how much is actually recoverable.}\n\\label{fig:oracle_scaling}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inverse scaling under adaptive thresholds.}\nIncreasing $N$ \\textit{decreases} construction F1 in 40 of 58 transitions ($p{<}0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nMethods using $\\theta{=}2/N$ are most affected ($-3.09$\\,pp at $N{=}8$, $-6.90$\\,pp at $N{=}16$ vs.\\ greedy on Few-NERD); majority vote ($\\theta{=}\\tfrac{1}{2}$) reduces the delta to $-0.87$/$-0.60$\\,pp---severity reduction, not immunity (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_sweep}).\nInverse scaling concentrates in non-degenerate instances ($11.1$\\% vs.\\ $1.3$\\% for degenerate; OR${=}0.109$; Appendix~\\ref{app:degen_inverse}).\nFew-shot demonstrations improve QE (CoNLL SJ $\\rho$ $.30{\\to}.44$) but not construction (all n.s.; Appendix~\\ref{app:fs_error}).\n\n\\subsection{Degeneracy Decomposition}\\label{sec:theory}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Degeneracy Decomposition.}\\label{thm:degeneracy_bound}\nFor any aggregation method $A$: $\\mathbb{E}[\\Delta F_1(A)] \\leq (1 - D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}}$,\nwhere $D$ is the degeneracy rate and $G_{\\textup{nd}}$ the oracle gap on non-degenerate instances.\nFor signal-based methods with alignment $\\alpha \\in [0,1]$, this tightens to $(1{-}D) \\cdot G_{\\textup{nd}} \\cdot \\alpha$ (proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:bound_proof}).\n\n\\noindent The bound is an intentionally loose \\textit{necessary condition test}: when $D{\\to}1$ or $\\alpha{\\to}0$, no aggregation method can improve over greedy, regardless of implementation.\nIt identifies \\textit{where} aggregation fails---screening for favorable conditions---rather than quantitatively predicting gains.\nAcross 16 configurations, all gains respect the bound; the low recovery ratio (3--12\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:bound}) reflects its role as a screening test, not a tight estimate.\n$\\alpha$---not $D$---is the binding constraint ($\\alpha{=}0.000$--$0.121$; $8$--$33{\\times}$ tighter), and this conclusion is robust to signal combination (\\S\\ref{sec:robustness_main}), verifier-based construction (\\S\\ref{sec:verifier}), and $\\theta_{\\text{crit}} \\in [0.60, 0.80]$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_bound}\n\\caption{SC gain vs.\\ degeneracy across 16 configurations (SciERC-specific $\\alpha$). Red: signal-adjusted bound ($\\alpha{=}0.068$); vertical dashed: $\\theta_{\\text{crit}}{=}0.70$; horizontal dotted: $\\varepsilon{=}0.3$\\,pp. All positive gains fall within the bound (right panel: ratio to weak bound, all ${<}0.13$).}\n\\label{fig:bound}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Bound looseness.}\nThe decomposition is a necessary condition screen showing how degeneracy and alignment jointly suppress gains, not a quantitative prediction tool. Two sources of looseness dominate: (1)~the entity independence assumption---split-half correlation $r{=}0.395$--$0.578$ (Table~\\ref{tab:fk_independence}) reveals substantial inter-entity correlation structure within instances, which the bound ignores; (2)~$\\alpha$ is estimated conservatively as the minimum across instances rather than an instance-adaptive value, further widening the gap. Together these explain the 3--12\\% recovery ratio (bound ratio $0.13$--$0.63$). The decomposition nonetheless identifies $\\alpha{\\to}0$, not degeneracy, as the binding constraint across all tested configurations.\n\nMV improves F1 only if marginal-entity precision exceeds $F_1^{\\textup{gr}}/2$ (Proposition~\\ref{prop:entity_selection_bound}; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_crit}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:alpha_crit} validates: at $N{=}8$, no dataset meets this condition ($\\Delta$ from $-2.74$ to ${\\approx}0$\\,pp); CoNLL ($\\alpha_{\\textup{crit,eff}}{=}1.148 {>} 1$) makes MV gain structurally impossible.\n",

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Experimental Setup}\label{sec:setup}
4	
5	\paragraph{Datasets.}
6	We evaluate on four English NER benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.
7	\textbf{SciERC} \cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test instances, 6 entity types) is the primary medium-difficulty test bed (${\approx}$65\% F1).
8	\textbf{Few-NERD} \cite{ding2021fewnerd} (INTRA coarse; 37{,}648 instances, 8 entity types, ${\approx}$75\% F1) provides a low-degeneracy contrast where LP selection succeeds (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}).
9	\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} (3{,}453 test sentences, 4 entity types, ${\approx}$91\% F1) serves as a ceiling-effect control.
10	\textbf{WNUT-17} \cite{derczynski2017wnut} (1{,}287 social media instances, 53.7\% zero F1) probes the signal-collapse boundary.
11	
12	\paragraph{Models and sampling.}
13	\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are LoRA fine-tuned using LLaMA Factory \cite{zheng2024llamafactory} (Appendix~\ref{app:training}; Few-NERD uses 3 epochs vs.\ 5 for others; Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}).
14	$N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary) with vLLM constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided}; $N {\in} \{16, 32, 64\}$ for scaling (Figure~\ref{fig:n_scaling}; Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
15	
16	\begin{figure}[t]
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_n_scaling_mv.pdf}
19	\caption{MV $\Delta$F1 vs.\ $\log_2 N$ across three datasets (Qwen3-8B FT, seed${=}42$). Dotted gray: $\Delta{=}0$. Gains are dataset-dependent: persistently negative on Few-NERD, near zero on SciERC at higher $N$, marginally positive on CoNLL ($+0.6$\,pp at $N{=}64$).}
20	\label{fig:n_scaling}
21	\end{figure}
22	
23	%% ============================================================
24	\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\label{sec:qe_results}
25	
26	\begin{table*}[t]
27	\centering
28	\small
29	\caption{NER quality estimation ($\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, primary seed${=}42$; 5-seed $\sigma{\leq}0.026$ in Table~\ref{tab:multiseed}). AUROC binarizes at per-dataset median F1. Best in \textbf{bold}. RE in Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension}; $N{=}16$ in Table~\ref{tab:robustness}.}
30	\label{tab:quality_estimation}
31	\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc}
32	\toprule
33	& & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{SciERC}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\
34	\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6} \cmidrule(lr){7-8}
35	Signal & Family & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC \\
36	\midrule
37	VC & Structural & \textbf{.379} & .684 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\
38	SJ  & Structural & .360 & .692 & .436 & .801 & \textbf{.431} & .704 \\
39	FK & Surface & .267 & .629 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\
40	EM  & Surface    & .295 & \textbf{.749} & \textbf{.451} & \textbf{.816} & \textbf{.431} & .705 \\
41	\midrule
42	LP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \textbf{.761} \\
43	\bottomrule
44	\end{tabular}
45	\end{table*}
46	
47	Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.
48	
49	\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}
50	On SciERC, structural signals lead (VC $\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.151{\pm}0.006$, 5 seeds; Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).
51	Under seed-replication Holm--Bonferroni correction ($k{=}5$ seeds), the SJ~${>}$~LP advantage reaches $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.013$ (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).
52	On CoNLL (F1${\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$).
53	At the signal-collapse boundary (WNUT-17, F1${\approx}$42\%), full-set correlations are dominated by the zero/non-zero boundary (VC $\rho{=}0.724$ at $N{=}16$, but conditional $\rho{=}0.038$; Table~\ref{tab:wnut}); LP shows the reverse (conditional $\rho{=}0.350$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}).
54	
55	%% ============================================================
56	\subsection{Cross-Dataset Contrast: Few-NERD}\label{sec:fewnerd}
57	
58	\begin{table}[t]
59	\centering
60	\small
61	\caption{LP selection across datasets (instance-averaged F1; Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, gold-filtered, 3-seed means). $p$: paired bootstrap.}
62	\label{tab:fewnerd}
63	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
64	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrccccr@{}}
65	\toprule
66	Dataset & $n$ & Greedy & Degen.\% & LP $\rho$ & LP $\Delta$ & $p$ \\
67	\midrule
68	WNUT-17 & 1{,}287 & .452 & 67.9\% & $-$.446 & $-$0.45\,pp & .72 \\
69	CoNLL & 2{,}756 & .908 & 61.7\% & .302 & $-$0.85\,pp & 1.0 \\
70	SciERC & 529 & .663 & 12.2\% & .159 & $+$0.61\,pp & .193 \\
71	Few-NERD & 32{,}565 & .748 & 13.0\% & .220 & $\mathbf{+1.39}$\,pp & ${<}.001$ \\
72	\bottomrule
73	\end{tabular}}
74	\end{table}
75	
76	Few-NERD (Table~\ref{tab:fewnerd}) confirms LP selection at low degeneracy ($+1.39$~pp\footnote{Under corpus micro-F1, LP $\Delta{=}+0.09{\pm}0.03$\,pp; the larger instance-averaged gains concentrate on few-entity instances.}, $p{<}0.001$; structural signals degrade: VC $-1.79$, SJ $-2.14$~pp), with LP 50$\times$ more seed-stable ($\sigma{=}0.04$ vs.\ SJ $2.02$~pp), generalizing to LLaMA (Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection}). Training convergence modulates degeneracy (5-epoch: $31$\%, LP $\Delta$ collapses from $+1.39$ to $+0.09$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}).
77	
78	Signal ranking is task-specific; per-type analysis shows MV degradation concentrates in low-F1 types ($\rho{=}0.90$, $p{=}0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:pertype}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Degeneracy alone does not predict LP direction.}
81	Degeneracy does not predict LP gain across 23 configurations ($\rho{=}0.052$, $p{=}0.81$; $n{=}23$, insufficient for reliable correlation---we treat this as supplementary evidence; Figure~\ref{fig:degeneracy_scatter}), though it co-varies with LP within fixed settings ($\rho{=}{-}0.881$; Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_lp}).
82	
83	\begin{figure}[t]
84	\centering
85	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_degeneracy_lp_scatter.pdf}
86	\caption{Instance degeneracy vs.\ LP selection gain across 23 configurations ($N{=}8$; $\rho{=}0.052$, $p{=}0.81$). Within Few-NERD per-entity-type ($\rho{=}{-}0.881$; Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_lp}), degeneracy predicts LP gain when alignment is held constant.}
87	\label{fig:degeneracy_scatter}
88	\end{figure}
89	
90	MV gains range from $-2.71$\,pp (Few-NERD) to $+0.6$\,pp (CoNLL at $N{=}64$; Figure~\ref{fig:n_scaling}); the optimal strategy is dataset-dependent (LP-best $+1.4$\,pp on Few-NERD vs.\ MV $+0.61$\,pp on CoNLL).
91	The framework extends to RE, where $40$\% zero-F1 from upstream entity span errors creates a distinct failure mode---\textit{diversity among failures} rather than quality-differentiated outputs---despite comparable degeneracy ($10.1$\% vs.\ SciERC $13.4$\%; Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension}).
92	
93	%% ============================================================
94	\subsection{Robustness}\label{sec:robustness_main}
95	
96	Signal rankings are robust across temperatures, seeds, and $N$ (Table~\ref{tab:robustness}; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}); Qwen3-4B QE matches 8B ($|\Delta\rho|{=}0.042$).
97	The gap persists from 4B to 72B zero-shot to GPT-5.5 (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
98	
99	%% ============================================================
100	\subsection{Entity Construction (Exploratory)}\label{sec:prescriptive}
101	
102	\paragraph{Entity-level construction.}
103	We aggregate entities across samples, retaining those exceeding $\theta{=}2/N$ with LP-weighted scoring ($\theta$ stable in $[0.15, 0.35]$; Appendix~\ref{app:theta_sweep}).
104	LP-weighted construction yields $+1.40{\pm}0.51$\,pp on Few-NERD (4-seed mean; $p{<}0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:entity_construction}), consistent with favorable diagnostic conditions ($D{=}13$\%, headroom${=}12.4$\,pp), a non-significant $+0.32$\,pp on SciERC, and no gain on CoNLL ($-0.39$\,pp).\footnote{Degenerate instances contribute $\Delta{=}0$ by construction; all gains concentrate in the non-degenerate subset.}
105	Under per-family Holm--Bonferroni \cite{holm1979simple} correction ($k{=}10$ entity-construction comparisons), $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.006$; global ($k{=}57$): $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.055$; the family partition was not pre-registered (Appendix~\ref{app:holm}).
106	Construction and LP selection are complementary ($\rho_{\text{adj}}$: Few-NERD $0.516$); QE signals support selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\,pp at 80\% coverage; Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection}).
107	Training convergence modulates returns: 5-epoch Few-NERD eliminates LP gains (selection: $+1.39{\to}+0.12$\,pp; construction: $+0.88{\to}+0.20$\,pp) as degeneracy rises from $13$\% to $31$\% (Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}).
108	
109	\phantomsection\label{sec:selection_results}
110	
111	\paragraph{Discussion and conclusion.}\label{sec:discussion}\label{sec:selection_method}\label{sec:conclusion}
112	For discrete-answer reasoning, sample diversity reflects diverse reasoning paths; for NER, degeneracy ($12$--$68$\%) reduces candidate diversity while signal alignment collapse ($\alpha{\to}0$) limits ranking.
113	The degeneracy decomposition (\S\ref{sec:theory}) identifies $\alpha$---not degeneracy---as the binding constraint ($8$--$33{\times}$ tighter); this persists across families \cite{yun2025price}, scales (4B--72B), and decoding modes (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).
114	The diagnostic framework---identifying these co-occurring conditions---is our primary contribution.
115	At $N{=}8$, high degeneracy ($>50$\%) suggests scaling will not help; low degeneracy with strong LP-F1 alignment may warrant exploratory entity construction; otherwise, greedy decoding remains the default.
116

Training convergence modulates degeneracy (5-epoch: $31$\\%, LP $\\Delta$ collapses from $+1.39$ to $+0.09$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:epoch_confound}).\n\nSignal ranking is task-specific; per-type analysis shows MV degradation concentrates in low-F1 types ($\\rho{=}0.90$, $p{=}0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:pertype}).\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneracy alone does not predict LP direction.}\nDegeneracy does not predict LP gain across 23 configurations ($\\rho{=}0.052$, $p{=}0.81$; $n{=}23$, insufficient for reliable correlation---we treat this as supplementary evidence; Figure~\\ref{fig:degeneracy_scatter}), though it co-varies with LP within fixed settings ($\\rho{=}{-}0.881$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_lp}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_degeneracy_lp_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Instance degeneracy vs.\\ LP selection gain across 23 configurations ($N{=}8$; $\\rho{=}0.052$, $p{=}0.81$). Within Few-NERD per-entity-type ($\\rho{=}{-}0.881$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_lp}), degeneracy predicts LP gain when alignment is held constant.}\n\\label{fig:degeneracy_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\nMV gains range from $-2.71$\\,pp (Few-NERD) to $+0.6$\\,pp (CoNLL at $N{=}64$; Figure~\\ref{fig:n_scaling}); the optimal strategy is dataset-dependent (LP-best $+1.4$\\,pp on Few-NERD vs.\\ MV $+0.61$\\,pp on CoNLL).\nThe framework extends to RE, where $40$\\% zero-F1 from upstream entity span errors creates a distinct failure mode---\\textit{diversity among failures} rather than quality-differentiated outputs---despite comparable degeneracy ($10.1$\\% vs.\\ SciERC $13.4$\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:re_extension}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nSignal rankings are robust across temperatures, seeds, and $N$ (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}); Qwen3-4B QE matches 8B ($|\\Delta\\rho|{=}0.042$).\nThe gap persists from 4B to 72B zero-shot to GPT-5.5 (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Entity Construction (Exploratory)}\\label{sec:prescriptive}\n\n\\paragraph{Entity-level construction.}\nWe aggregate entities across samples, retaining those exceeding $\\theta{=}2/N$ with LP-weighted scoring ($\\theta$ stable in $[0.15, 0.35]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:theta_sweep}).\nLP-weighted construction yields $+1.40{\\pm}0.51$\\,pp on Few-NERD (4-seed mean; $p{<}0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:entity_construction}), consistent with favorable diagnostic conditions ($D{=}13$\\%, headroom${=}12.4$\\,pp), a non-significant $+0.32$\\,pp on SciERC, and no gain on CoNLL ($-0.39$\\,pp).\\footnote{Degenerate instances contribute $\\Delta{=}0$ by construction; all gains concentrate in the non-degenerate subset.}\nUnder per-family Holm--Bonferroni \\cite{holm1979simple} correction ($k{=}10$ entity-construction comparisons), $p_{\\text{adj}}{=}0.006$; global ($k{=}57$): $p_{\\text{adj}}{=}0.055$; the family partition was not pre-registered (Appendix~\\ref{app:holm}).\nConstruction and LP selection are complementary ($\\rho_{\\text{adj}}$: Few-NERD $0.516$); QE signals support selective prediction ($+5$--$8$\\,pp at 80\\% coverage; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nTraining convergence modulates returns: 5-epoch Few-NERD eliminates LP gains (selection: $+1.39{\\to}+0.12$\\,pp; construction: $+0.88{\\to}+0.20$\\,pp) as degeneracy rises from $13$\\% to $31$\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:epoch_confound}).\n\n\\phantomsection\\label{sec:selection_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Discussion and conclusion.}\\label{sec:discussion}\\label{sec:selection_method}\\label{sec:conclusion}\nFor discrete-answer reasoning, sample diversity reflects diverse reasoning paths; for NER, degeneracy ($12$--$68$\\%) reduces candidate diversity while signal alignment collapse ($\\alpha{\\to}0$) limits ranking.\nThe degeneracy decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}) identifies $\\alpha$---not degeneracy---as the binding constraint ($8$--$33{\\times}$ tighter); this persists across families \\cite{yun2025price}, scales (4B--72B), and decoding modes (Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}).\nThe diagnostic framework---identifying these co-occurring conditions---is our primary contribution.\nAt $N{=}8$, high degeneracy ($>50$\\%) suggests scaling will not help; low degeneracy with strong LP-F1 alignment may warrant exploratory entity construction; otherwise, greedy decoding remains the default.\n",

_source line 2328 · 2026-05-23T13:52:06.562Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me evaluate the figure:

**Positive aspects:**
1. Three-panel structure is clear and follows the paper's "Discover → Diagnose → Construct" narrative
2. Color coding (blue, orange, green) provides good visual differentiation
3. The "Correlation-Selection Gap" red banner clearly labels the key concept
4. "Resolution" green banner connects to entity construction
5. AUROC=0.82 and ρ=0.39 are displayed in Panel 1
6. Panel 2 effectively shows why sample selection fails (S=0.73 same score for all)


7. Panel 3 displays the entity grid with frequency information and the θ=2/N threshold, with the +1.4 pp improvement prominently featured

**Potential concerns:**
1. **Resolution at 300 DPI**: The text is quite small; at column-width in a double-column paper, this might be hard to read. The figure is 16×5.6 inches at 300 DPI, but it'll be scaled down to column-width (~3.3 inches) or text-width (~7 inches). At text-width, it should be fine.
2. **Font size**: Some labels like "Predicting Reliability" and "Choosing Best Output" might need to be larger for readability at the final printed size.
3. **"S" score box**: Effectively highlights the signal visually.
4. **Entity grid in Panel 3**: The PER/LOC/ORG categories are clear, though they could be difficult to read when scaled down.
5. **Visual hierarchy**: The red and green banners connecting the panels work well to guide the eye.

Now checking the numerical values against the paper's reported results—the AUROC of 0.82 aligns with the EM AUROC on CoNLL, the ρ=0.39 matches the VC correlation on SciERC when rounded, and the +1.4 pp improvement corresponds to the Few-NERD construction results.

A couple of things to keep in mind: the figure's width might cause text to become illegible when scaled to column width in the final layout, and the S=0.73 value in Panel 2 should be understood as illustrative rather than a specific data point. Overall, the figure communicates the conceptual flow clearly and should work well for submission, even if it lacks the polish of a professionally designed graphic.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see Figure 1 on page 3. Let me evaluate:

**Visual quality assessment:**
1. **Three-panel layout**: Clear horizontal flow (Quality Estimation → Sample Selection → Entity-Level Construction)
2. **Color coding**: Blue (QE), Orange/Yellow (Selection), Green (Construction) — consistent and distinguishable
3. **Top banners**: Red "Correlation-Selection Gap" and green "Resolution" arrows connecting panels — effective narrative flow
4. **Text legibility**: At text-width in the paper, the text is quite small but readable. The key numbers (AUROC=0.82, ρ=0.39, S=0.73, +1.4 pp) are visible.


5. **Caption details**: The figure shows consistency signals estimating instance quality with correlations up to 0.45, instance-level signals that assign identical scores preventing selection improvement over greedy, and LP-weighted entity construction with a conditional gain of +1.4 pp on Few-NERD. The caption notes this is a diagnostic sequence rather than a causal chain, using Qwen3-8B fine-tuned on Few-NERD with N=8 and T=1.0.

**Numerical accuracy in figure:**
- AUROC=0.82: The paper's Table 4 shows CoN LL EM AUROC=0.816, which rounds to 0.82. But looking more carefully at the metrics, I'm seeing some discrepancies—the figure reports AUROC=0.82 and ρ=0.39, while Table 4 shows SciERC with VC ρ=0.379 and AUC=0.684, which doesn't quite match what's displayed.

The caption mentions "Qwen3-8B fine-tuned, Few-NERD, N=8, T=1.0" but Few-NERD doesn't appear directly in Table 4, which only covers SciERC and CoNLL datasets. The quality estimation numbers for Few-NERD might be elsewhere in the appendix or a different table. Looking at the appendix tables and registry data, the Few-NERD values don't align with the figure's ρ=0.39—the experiments show Soft Jaccard ρ around 0.31, while the seed 42 results show conditional ρ of 0.45 but logprob ρ only at 0.20 with AUROC of 0.60, which also doesn't match the figure's AUROC=0.82. I'm noticing a discrepancy — the figure shows 0.82 AUROC for Few-NERD, but when I check the actual experiment logs, the logprob AUROC for Few-NERD seed 42 comes out to around 0.60, not 0.82. This suggests the figure might be using illustrative or representative values rather than the exact reported metrics, which is worth flagging as a potential inconsistency. I'm noticing the +1.4 pp improvement in Panel 3 actually matches the Few-NERD LP construction result, and since the caption explicitly states this is Few-NERD data, the AUROC=0.82 should correspond to an actual metric from that dataset. I've checked the N=32 diagnostics and found instance-level AUROC around 0.60, but entity-level metrics might differ, and the specific value could be in an appendix table I haven't reviewed yet—I'll flag this as something to verify rather than dig deeper.

Now I'm stepping back to assess the overall figure quality: the three-panel design flows well conceptually with clear visual hierarchy, the matplotlib rendering is adequate for submission though not as polished as professional tools, colors are consistent and accessible, and most labels are readable at text width. The main thing to verify before posting is whether those numerical values actually correspond to Few-NERD results as claimed.
```

542:Cross-dataset transfer to Few-NERD is negative: $-0.76$\,pp (B2-v2), $-1.02$\,pp (B2-v3), $-1.27$\,pp (B2-v4 Replace, AUC${=}0.822$), and $-0.85$\,pp (B2-v4 Augment, AUC${=}0.855$), all below greedy.
627:A logistic regression on mean-pooled final-layer hidden states at entity spans achieves AUROC $0.636$ (Few-NERD), $0.882$ (CoNLL), $0.630$ (SciERC), evaluated with GroupKFold CV (5-fold ${\times}$ 3 seeds, groups${=}$source instance to prevent entity-level leakage; \S\ref{app:hidden_probe}).
629:Cross-dataset transfer is weak: leave-one-dataset-out AUROC drops to $0.559$ (Few-NERD${\to}$CoNLL) and $0.671$ (CoNLL${\to}$Few-NERD), indicating that entity error patterns are dataset-specific rather than reflecting a universal introspection boundary, limiting probe-based approaches without per-dataset calibration.
683:ESJ achieves near-random AUROC on all datasets, confirming that the self-verification failure observed on Few-NERD (\S\ref{sec:mechanistic}) generalizes.
1462:Qwen  & RE  & 8  & 0.822 & VC $>$ LP $>$ FK $>$ SJ $>$ EM \\
1939:\caption{Few-NERD INTRA coarse: complete quality estimation and selection results (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, gold-filtered $n{=}32{,}565$). $\rho$: Spearman correlation with instance F1. Sample AUROC: per-sample LP discrimination. Sel.\ $\Delta$: selection F1 minus greedy (0.748). CI: bootstrap 95\% confidence interval.}

1935	
1936	\begin{table}[t]
1937	\centering
1938	\small
1939	\caption{Few-NERD INTRA coarse: complete quality estimation and selection results (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, gold-filtered $n{=}32{,}565$). $\rho$: Spearman correlation with instance F1. Sample AUROC: per-sample LP discrimination. Sel.\ $\Delta$: selection F1 minus greedy (0.748). CI: bootstrap 95\% confidence interval.}
1940	\label{tab:fewnerd_full}
1941	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
1942	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
1943	\toprule
1944	Signal & $\rho$ & Sample AUROC & Sel.\ $\Delta$ & 95\% CI \\
1945	\midrule
1946	LP & .220 & .578 & $\mathbf{+1.39}$\,pp & [+1.18, +1.53] \\
1947	VC & .386 & --- & $-$1.79\,pp & --- \\
1948	FK & .313 & --- & $-$2.26\,pp & --- \\
1949	SJ & .300 & --- & $-$2.14\,pp & --- \\
1950	EM & .287 & --- & $-$3.85\,pp & --- \\
1951	\midrule
1952	\multicolumn{3}{l}{Greedy: 0.748 \quad Oracle: 0.872} & \multicolumn{2}{l}{Headroom: 12.4\,pp} \\
1953	\bottomrule
1954	\end{tabular}
1955	}
1956	\end{table}
1957	
1958	\paragraph{Key observations.}
1959	(1)~All five signals achieve positive QE ($\rho$ 0.22--0.39); quality estimation works on Few-NERD. VC leads ($\rho{=}0.39$), followed by FK ($0.31$), SJ ($0.30$), EM ($0.29$), LP ($0.22$); the ranking matches SciERC where structural signals also outperform LP for quality estimation.
1960	(2)~LP is the only signal achieving significant positive selection ($+1.39$~pp, $p{<}0.001$, CI excludes zero). The sample-level AUROC of 0.578 confirms LP provides per-sample discriminability beyond instance-level quality prediction.
1961	(3)~Despite positive $\rho$, all structural signals degrade selection ($-1.79$ to $-3.85$~pp), further confirming that the correlation-selection gap is not an artifact of weak QE.
1962	(4)~Low degeneracy (13.0\%) combined with strong LP-quality alignment ($\rho{\approx}0.5$--$0.7$) enables LP selection: candidates differ in F1 more often, and LP can rank them effectively. This contrasts with SciERC (12.2\% degeneracy but weak alignment $\rho{\approx}0.16$, LP $+0.61$~pp) and CoNLL (61.7\%, LP $-0.85$~pp).
1963	
1964	\section{Per-Entity-Type LP Selection Analysis}\label{app:per_type_lp}
1965	
1966	Table~\ref{tab:per_type_lp} stratifies LP selection by entity type on Few-NERD, sorted by degeneracy rate.
1967	The degeneracy-gating mechanism holds at entity-type granularity: 7 of 8 types show positive LP selection gains, and the sole negative type (Art, $-0.17$~pp) has the highest degeneracy (19.8\%).
1968	The correlation between per-type degeneracy and oracle headroom is strong and significant (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.881$, $p{=}0.004$; exact permutation $p{=}0.005$); degeneracy directly constrains the headroom available for selection.
1969	Under a conservative $\text{df}{=}4$ adjustment for within-type dependence, the correlation remains significant ($p{=}0.033$).
1970	Entity types show near-zero intra-class correlation (ICC $= -0.004$), supporting the independence assumption underlying per-type analysis.
1971	
1972	\begin{table}[h]
1973	\centering
1974	\small
1975	\caption{Per-entity-type LP selection on Few-NERD INTRA coarse (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$), sorted by degeneracy. LP $\Delta$: LP selection F1 minus greedy. 7/8 types show positive $\Delta$; Art (highest degeneracy) is the sole exception.}
1976	\label{tab:per_type_lp}
1977	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
1978	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
1979	\toprule
1980	Entity Type & $n$ & Degen.\% & Greedy & LP Sel & $\Delta$ (pp) & LP $\rho$ & Headroom \\
1981	\midrule
1982	Event & 1{,}306 & 6.0 & .655 & .662 & $+$0.65 & .457 & 16.1\,pp \\
1983	Other & 3{,}433 & 6.6 & .637 & .685 & $\mathbf{+4.73}$ & .491 & 19.4\,pp \\
1984	Organization & 6{,}647 & 6.7 & .720 & .732 & $+$1.21 & .509 & 13.3\,pp \\

LP $\\Delta$: LP selection F1 minus greedy. 7/8 types show positive $\\Delta$; Art (highest degeneracy) is the sole exception.}\n\\label{tab:per_type_lp}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nEntity Type & $n$ & Degen.\\% & Greedy & LP Sel & $\\Delta$ (pp) & LP $\\rho$ & Headroom \\\\\n\\midrule\nEvent & 1{,}306 & 6.0 & .655 & .662 & $+$0.65 & .457 & 16.1\\,pp \\\\\nOther & 3{,}433 & 6.6 & .637 & .685 & $\\mathbf{+4.73}$ & .491 & 19.4\\,pp \\\\\nOrganization & 6{,}647 & 6.7 & .720 & .732 & $+$1.21 & .509 & 13.3\\,pp \\\\",

30	
31	We exclude instances with empty gold annotations from all correlation analyses (CoNLL: 697/3{,}453 instances, 20.2\%; SciERC: 22/551, 4.0\%).
32	For these instances, F1 is trivially zero regardless of prediction quality, and consistency signals reflect agreement on the empty set rather than extraction quality. Including them inflates or deflates Spearman $\rho$ depending on signal behavior at the boundary.

So, my conclusion stands: **AUROC=0.82 in Figure 1 has no direct data support from any Few-NERD experiment**. The figure caption explicitly states it's for "Few-NERD, N=8, T=1.0" but uses a value that doesn't appear in any Few-NERD table.

The closest values are:
- CoNLL EM AUC = 0.816 (from Table 4) — close but wrong dataset


- Few-NERD entity-level agree_auroc = 0.881 (from ESJ experiments) — different metric
- Few-NERD cross-dataset transfer AUC = 0.822 (from appendix) — negative result

The ρ=0.39 checks out—it matches Few-NERD VC at 0.386—but the AUROC=0.82 remains unaccounted for, whether it's from a missing table, a different metric, or possibly misreported.

The caption's reference to "ρ up to 0.45" actually points to Table 4's maximum across all datasets (EM on CoNLL Qwen = 0.451), not the figure's ρ=0.39, so there's an inconsistency in which results the figure and caption are highlighting. This is a warning-level concern because if someone checks the Few-NERD AUROC, they'll find 0.578 (LP Sample), not 0.82—which could look like data cherry-picking or misrepresentation. I should flag this finding now.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014VCPW5j5Kkf3huedA1ectG`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Diagnostic Framework}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	Our analysis is observational: we characterize statistical associations between agreement signals and output quality without claiming causal mechanisms.
4	
5	\subsection{Problem Formulation}\label{sec:formulation}
6	
7	Given model $\mathcal{M}$ and input $x$, we sample $N$ outputs $\{y_1, \ldots, y_N\}$ at temperature $T > 0$ via constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided}, where each $y_i$ is an entity set.
8	A \textbf{per-sample signal} $c_{\text{ps}}: (x, y_i) \to \mathbb{R}$ scores each output independently; an \textbf{instance-level signal} $c_{\text{il}}: (x, \{y_i\}_{i=1}^N) \to \mathbb{R}$ produces a single score per input (notation in Appendix~\ref{app:notation}).
9	This distinction is consequential: 4 of 5 signals are instance-level aggregates assigning identical scores to all $N$ candidates.
10	Only LP carries per-sample discriminative power: ensemble optimization assigns LP mean weight $0.78$, with structural signals receiving zero weight (Appendix~\ref{app:supervised_combination}).
11	When LP fails---as in few-shot regimes where LP $\rho{\to}0$ (\S\ref{sec:scaling})---no alternative per-sample signal exists.
12	
13	We define the \textbf{capability ceiling} as the practical performance upper bound achievable through self-consistency at realistic sample budgets ($N{\leq}16$), beyond which diminishing returns render further scaling impractical.
14	
15	\subsection{Confidence Signals}\label{sec:signals}
16	
17	Entity matching uses exact span and type match.
18	We distinguish two signal families: four \textit{agreement signals} computed across samples---\textit{structural} (Soft Jaccard [SJ], entity voting confidence [VC]) and \textit{surface} (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\kappa$ [FK])---and log-probability [LP] as the sole \textit{model-internal} signal, derived from generation probabilities and providing per-sample discrimination.
19	
20	\paragraph{Log-probability (per-sample).}
21	$c_{\text{lp}}(y_i) = |y_i|^{-1} \sum_{j} \log p_{\mathcal{M}}(t_j \mid t_{<j}, x)$.
22	Mean LP is preferred over cumulative sequence-level LP as length normalization eliminates length bias ($+4.6$ F1 points; Appendix~\ref{app:token_signal}).
23	
24	\paragraph{Soft Jaccard (structural, instance-level).}
25	For samples $y_i, y_j$, we group by type, compute max-weight bipartite matching (Hungarian algorithm, token-level IoU):
26	\begin{equation}\label{eq:sj}
27	\text{SJ}(y_i, y_j) = \frac{\sum_{m \in M} \text{sim}(a_m, b_m)}{\sum_{g} \max(|g_i|, |g_j|)}
28	\end{equation}
29	Instance-level QE averages over all pairs: $c_{\text{sj}}(x) = \binom{N}{2}^{-1} \sum_{i<j} \text{SJ}(y_i, y_j)$.
30	For selection, consensus-proximity converts to per-sample rankings via MBR (\S\ref{sec:mbr_theory}).
31	
32	\paragraph{Other signals.}
33	EM: proportion of samples producing the most common output ($c_{\text{em}}(x) = N^{-1} \max_{y} |\{i : y_i = y\}|$).
34	FK: inter-rater agreement \cite{fleiss1971agreement} adapted to entity sets, treating unique (text, type) tuples as items with binary present/absent labels (Appendix~\ref{app:fk_independence}).
35	VC: mean entity frequency $\text{freq}(e) = |\{i : e \in y_i\}| / N$ across $E_\cup$.\footnote{When $E_\cup = \emptyset$, SJ${=}1$ (perfect agreement on empty output); $\text{VC}{=}0$.}
36	
37	\subsection{Evaluation}\label{sec:evaluation}
38	
39	\paragraph{Quality estimation.}
40	Spearman $\rho$ between signal and instance-level F1 (exact span-and-type match), and AUROC classifying above/below median F1.
41	Both exclude instances with no gold annotations: $n{=}529$ on SciERC, $n{=}2{,}756$ on CoNLL (Appendix~\ref{app:additional}).
42	
43	\paragraph{Sample selection.}
44	Best-of-$N$ F1: LP ranks candidates directly; instance-level signals rank via consensus-proximity.
45	Baselines: \textit{oracle} (highest-F1 sample) and \textit{greedy} ($T{=}0$).
46	\textbf{Instance degeneracy}: fraction of instances where all $N$ samples yield identical F1;\footnote{``Identical F1'' means constant F1 across all $N$ samples for a given instance, not necessarily F1${=}1.0$. This includes three distinct cases: (1)~identical entity sets (true output collapse), (2)~different entity sets that happen to achieve the same F1 (coincidental F1 tie), and (3)~different entity boundaries with identical span-level scores. Entity-set exact-match degeneracy (case 1 only) is lower ($9$--$26$\% vs.\ $13$--$31$\% constant-F1; Appendix~\ref{app:epoch_confound}); we use constant-F1 as the operationally relevant definition since selection cannot improve F1 regardless of which case applies.} for these, no signal can improve selection.
47	We adopt \textit{degeneracy} (from statistical mechanics: multiple microstates mapping to identical macrostates) to distinguish from training convergence and to emphasize the many-to-one collapse of distinct samples onto identical entity sets.
48	
```

### Tool result

_source line 2394 · 2026-05-23T14:29:43.022Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01F57EtxEDasGDqURs472aHg`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Test-time compute scaling---repeated sampling with self-consistency or best-of-$N$---yields large gains on discrete-answer reasoning, but whether these gains transfer to NER remains open.
3	We characterize when scaling succeeds and fails, using NER (four datasets, primarily 7--14B fine-tuned) as the primary analysis, with preliminary cross-task pilots (RE, SQL).
4	Quality estimation signals predict output quality (AUROC up to $0.82$), yet instance-level selection yields no detectable gains (${<}1$\,pp; CI crosses zero), revealing a \textbf{correlation-selection gap}.
5	A diagnostic framework identifies two co-occurring conditions---\textit{degeneracy} (i.e., multiple outputs collapsing to identical predictions) and signal alignment collapse---that are jointly associated with scaling returns; cross-task exploration confirms task-specific failure modes.
6	Entity-level construction yields conditional gains ($+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD under 3-epoch training; collapses at convergence; global Holm $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.055$, suggestive; per-family $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.006$, post-hoc family partition) but exhibits inverse scaling in most configurations.
7	The framework is primarily diagnostic, with limited predictive power for identifying favorable conditions, informing resource allocation decisions before investing in repeated inference.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

</details>

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Test-time compute scaling---self-consistency \cite{wang2023selfconsistency}, best-of-$N$ \cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}, extended reasoning \cite{muennighoff2025s1}---yields large gains on discrete-answer tasks where outputs admit equivalence classes or execution-based verification.
4	For NER, where outputs are entity sets with partial overlap, the boundary conditions for effective scaling are unknown; prior work reports task-level correlations \cite{yao2026consistency} and qualitative diversity collapse \cite{yun2025price} but no instance-level diagnostic framework.
5	
6	Using NER (four datasets, primarily 7--14B fine-tuned; tentative cross-scale observations to 72B) and RE as case studies, we identify a \textbf{correlation-selection gap}: quality estimation signals predict output quality ($\rho$ up to $0.45$, AUROC $0.82$; Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}), yet selection yields no detectable gains---MV ${<}1$\,pp even at $N{=}64$ (Figure~\ref{fig:n_scaling}; Table~\ref{tab:selection}).
7	This gap decomposes into a definitional layer---four instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates---and an empirical one: LP, the only per-sample signal, also yields no gains, through within-instance compression (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}).
8	A hidden-state probe confirms models encode entity quality internally (AUROC $0.63$\textendash$0.88$; Appendix~\ref{sec:mechanistic}), exposing an \textit{introspection boundary} \cite{huang2024large}.
9	A \textit{diagnostic framework} localizes the failure to two co-occurring conditions---degeneracy ($12$--$68$\%) and signal alignment collapse ($\alpha{\to}0$; $\alpha$ here denotes LP-F1 alignment)---which co-occur with selection failure (Table~\ref{tab:method_summary}).
10	The framework decomposes observed failures post-hoc rather than predicting which instances will fail a priori; our analysis is observational and all reported associations are correlational, not causal.
11	The optimal strategy is dataset-dependent (LP $+1.39$\,pp on Few-NERD; MV $+0.61$\,pp on CoNLL), and RE reveals a distinct bottleneck ($40$\% zero-F1; Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension}).
12	Exploratory entity-level construction yields conditional improvement ($+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD, 3-epoch; global Holm $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.055$, suggestive; per-family $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.006$, post-hoc family partition; diminishes at convergence) where the framework identifies favorable conditions---low degeneracy and LP-F1 alignment---but inverse scaling in most $N$-transitions confirms that scaling is harmful in the majority of configurations.
13	
14	These findings rest on four NER benchmarks, two model families with 4B--72B ablations, $N {\in} \{2, \ldots, 64\}$, and exploratory extensions to RE\footnote{RE F1 is confounded by entity span errors: the majority of zero-F1 RE instances reflect upstream NER mismatches rather than relation classification failure (Appendix~\ref{app:re_extension}).} and SQL parsing ($87.2$\% degeneracy).
15	
16	\begin{figure*}[t]
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview}
19	\caption{Overview of the correlation-selection gap (diagnostic sequence, not causal chain). Representative values across datasets: AUROC from CoNLL EM; $\rho$ and entity-construction gain from Few-NERD (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$; Tables~\ref{tab:quality_estimation},~\ref{tab:entity_construction}). \textbf{Left}: consistency signals estimate instance quality. \textbf{Center}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all $N$ candidates, so selection cannot outperform greedy. \textbf{Right}: LP-weighted entity construction ($\theta{=}2/N$) yields conditional gains ($+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD; \S\ref{sec:prescriptive}).}
20	\label{fig:overview}
21	\end{figure*}
22	
23	Our investigation proceeds in three stages:
24	\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
25	\item \textbf{Discover} [\S\ref{sec:qe_results}].
26	QE signals achieve AUROC up to $0.82$; while \citet{yao2026consistency} report task-level correlation ($\rho$ up to 0.47), our instance-level decomposition reveals why correlation does not yield selection gains.
27	\item \textbf{Diagnose} [\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}, \S\ref{sec:adaptive}, \S\ref{sec:theory}].
28	A diagnostic cascade---degeneracy, LP compression, signal alignment---quantifies failure mechanisms at entity level; \citet{yun2025price} describe diversity collapse qualitatively, we provide quantitative decomposition ($D$, $\alpha$, LP range). A necessary condition bound (\S\ref{sec:theory}) confirms $\alpha$ as the binding constraint (3--12\% recovery ratio). Hidden-state probes reveal an introspection boundary (AUROC $0.63$--$0.88$ vs.\ ESJ $\approx 0.50$; Appendix~\ref{sec:mechanistic}).
29	\item \textbf{Construct (exploratory)} [\S\ref{sec:prescriptive}].
30	Entity-level construction yields $+1.40$\,pp on Few-NERD where the cascade identifies favorable conditions. Unlike \citet{xie2023zeroshot}'s zero-shot entity voting, we aggregate stochastic samples from fine-tuned models.
31	\end{enumerate}
32	
33	The paper is organized as follows: \S\ref{sec:related} contextualizes our contributions relative to prior art; \S\ref{sec:method} defines the diagnostic framework and confidence signals; \S\ref{sec:selection_gap} establishes the correlation-selection gap; \S\ref{sec:adaptive} evaluates adaptive methods; \S\ref{sec:theory_scaling} provides scaling analysis and degeneracy decomposition; \S\ref{sec:experiments} presents the main experimental results.
34	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related}
2	
3	\paragraph{Test-time scaling and self-consistency.}\label{sec:yun_contrast}
4	Self-consistency \cite{wang2023selfconsistency} and best-of-$N$ with scaling laws \cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys, gao2023scaling} yield large gains on reasoning; recent extensions include reward-tail-guided search \cite{li2026rewardtail}. Reward-free selectors \cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} and trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, lightman2024verify, li2023stepaware} target discrete-answer settings.
5	SC can fail when consistent outputs are wrong \cite{chen2023twofailures}; free-form \cite{chen2024usc} and atomic \cite{thirukovalluru2024atomic} variants face limits where sub-output agreement does not yield selection gain. Iterative self-verification \cite{shinn2023reflexion} offers an alternative, but our ESJ and MRSC results (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}) show such approaches also fail for NER.
6	\citet{yun2025price} identify diversity collapse under constrained decoding; we extend their qualitative account with quantitative diagnostics (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Consistency for structured IE.}
9	\citet{yao2026consistency} show consistency correlates with accuracy for LLM-based NER/RE ($\rho$ up to 0.47); we extend to instance-level analysis: (1)~task-level $\rho$ masks instance-level degeneracy; (2)~we decompose failure into degeneracy and LP compression; (3)~pre-deployment diagnostics that task-level correlation cannot provide.
10	Critically, their task-level granularity cannot distinguish instances where scaling helps from those where it harms---our entity-level decomposition addresses this gap (Table~\ref{tab:novelty_comparison}).
11	\citet{xie2023zeroshot} propose entity voting for zero-shot NER; \citet{xie2024selfimproving} report positive SC where zero-shot diversity is high.
12	
13	\paragraph{Uncertainty estimation and MBR.}
14	Our selection instantiates MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling}; \citet{eikema2025structured} find MBR utilities degrade on structured outputs, consistent with our LP compression findings.
15	LP is well-calibrated at population level \cite{kadavath2022language, tian2023calibration} and motivated as a quality signal by noisy channel models \cite{yee2019noisy}, yet too compressed within instances for selection.
16	Complementary approaches include uncertainty estimation for autoregressive structured prediction \cite{malinin2021uncertainty}, calibration of pre-trained transformers \cite{desai2020calibration}, large-scale uncertainty quantification \cite{xiao2022uncertainty}, semantic uncertainty \cite{kuhn2023semantic}, selective prediction \cite{geifman2017selective}, and conformal prediction \cite{angelopoulos2023conformal}.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Novelty comparison with closest related work along six dimensions.}
22	\label{tab:novelty_comparison}
23	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
24	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
25	\toprule
26	Dimension & \citet{yao2026consistency} & \citet{yun2025price} & \citet{landesberg2026bon} & Ours \\
27	\midrule
28	Domain & NER/RE & Reasoning & Reasoning & NER/RE/SQL \\
29	Granularity & Task & Task & Prompt & Instance \\
30	Failure mechanism & --- & Diversity collapse & Baseline effects & Degeneracy $+$ $\alpha$ \\
31	Diagnostic framework & $\rho$ only & Qualitative & $r$ global/local & $D$, $G_{\text{nd}}$, $\alpha$, LP \\
32	Scale range & Single & --- & Single & 4B--72B+API \\
33	Prescriptive & No & No & No & Conditional ($p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.055$) \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}}
36	\end{table}
37	
38	\paragraph{Concurrent work.}
39	\citet{landesberg2026bon} independently document a correlation-selection gap in best-of-$N$ reasoning: global judge $r{=}0.47$ masks within-prompt $r{=}0.27$ (Table~\ref{tab:novelty_comparison}).
40	This concurrent discovery validates the gap as cross-domain.
41	The studies diverge in four respects (Table~\ref{tab:novelty_comparison}):
42	\textit{(a)~domain}---reasoning (scalar judgments) vs.\ NER/RE (entity sets with partial overlap);
43	\textit{(b)~failure mechanism}---prompt-level baseline effects vs.\ degeneracy ($12$--$68$\%) and signal alignment collapse ($\alpha{\to}0$), mechanisms specific to structured prediction;
44	\textit{(c)~granularity}---prompt-level $r$ decomposition vs.\ instance- and entity-level diagnostics ($D$, $G_{\textup{nd}}$, $\alpha$);
45	\textit{(d)~prescriptive}---no constructive remedy vs.\ entity-level construction with MBR grounding (Proposition~\ref{prop:construction_mbr}).
46	
```

Good progress. Let me continue reading the remaining sections and the latest PDF. I see that:

1. **Figure 1 caption (introduction.tex:19)** has been updated to: "Representative values across datasets: AUROC from CoNLL EM; ρ and entity-construction gain from Few-NERD (Qwen3-8B, N=8, T=1.0; Tables 4, entity_construction)." This addresses my warning about AUROC=0.82 not being from Few-NERD.

2. **Abstract (abstract.tex)**: Let me count the words. I need to check if it's ≤200 words.
   
3. Now I'm checking the introduction for those AUROC 0.82 values at lines 6 and 26 to verify they're properly sourced, then I'll need to count the abstract word count, scan for undefined references in the log, and validate the key numerical claims throughout the paper.

Looking at the abstract more carefully, I'm seeing the core findings: quality estimation signals reach AUROC 0.82 but instance-level selection doesn't produce meaningful gains, pointing to a correlation-selection gap. The framework identifies degeneracy and signal alignment collapse as co-occurring conditions tied to scaling performance, with entity-level construction showing conditional improvements under specific training regimes but inverse scaling in most cases. Quality estimation signals achieve strong predictive performance with AUROC up to 0.82, but when applied to select individual instances, they fail to produce meaningful gains—the confidence interval crosses zero—exposing a gap between correlation and actual selection utility. I'm identifying two underlying failure modes: degeneracy where multiple outputs converge to identical predictions, and signal alignment collapse, both of which appear to drive the scaling returns across tasks.

Entity-level construction shows conditional promise with +1.40 pp gains on Few-NERD during early training, though this advantage disappears at convergence and reverses in most other configurations. The diagnostic framework reveals these task-specific failure patterns but has limited predictive power for determining when selection will actually help before committing to expensive inference runs.
